Then eight corrections, the first five the spike's, ported with their behaviour: a flow end that runs through a foreign symbol docks from the side and runs outside both; an inner piece on a foreign symbol moves beside it; steps of 1 to 5 px are smoothed; of two flows that leave a gateway at one corner the one that turns takes the free corner; an arrow in and an arrow out at one place on a task move apart; the second of two flows between the same two nodes runs around both, below them, 20 px beyond every symbol of their row within the span, not only beyond the two, and beyond an earlier flow of the pair that keeps Mermaid's route, which may already run 20 px beyond the row (story 2.21), the third above; a flow that arrives at the vertex of a gateway another flow leaves by, which reads as one double-headed arrow, first joins another flow arriving at that gateway, as a merge drawn by hand: one of its runs that meets the other flow's last piece at least 12 px from its vertex goes on to that piece, and the flow runs along it into the vertex; otherwise it docks on a free vertex, the one on the side it comes from first, 12 px beyond it and into it, or straight from its run before where that already passes over the vertex; otherwise it stays (Ben, 2026-10-04: a crossing beside the gateway and a double-headed arrow are both bad); a way a foreign symbol blocks, whose new pieces cross or lie on another flow (the joined one but along its last piece) or start a new pair closer than 12 px beside one (a pair the flow had may grow longer), is not taken, nor a free vertex that faces the target of a flow leaving the gateway, and an end moved counts at its new vertex for the next (`dockApart()`, story 2.21; the flows back are left out of it on both sides, since their ends are placed after it); and, after those (story 2.20), so that it keeps off their rings, a flow back within one row, its target left of its source at the same height, runs around the row instead of through it: out of the top or the bottom of its source, along a level 20 px beyond the row's symbols within its span, into the top or the bottom of its target (a circle and a diamond at their top or bottom, a task at the middle of the side, or, where another flow docks at the middle, 20 px in from a corner, the one with fewer conflicts, at a tie the one facing the other end; a gateway whose vertex there another flow uses at a side corner instead, 12 px out and then to its level, the corner facing the other end first and the one away from it, the ring running out and back beyond the gateway on its side of the row, at one conflict more, story 2.21); a twin routed before is left as it is. Flows back taken shortest first; one whose span overlaps that of one placed before on the same side takes the next level out, 16 px farther (measured on a process with 57 nodes: at 12 px the lines and their labels ran together), so a nested one lies inside and no two share a line; disjoint spans share the innermost level. Of the two sides the one with fewer conflicts at its level wins (a piece of another flow crossed, shared or run beside closer than 12 px, a port of a gateway or an event another flow uses), up to three levels farther out where that has fewer; at a tie the side Mermaid's route kept to; a way a foreign symbol blocks is not taken. The side is chosen with every end counted as using its port; on that side the ports are chosen again with the ends that stay (story 2.21, Ben's decisions after its second review): at the source a flow out that `fanOut()` moves to a free corner once the ring leaves by its vertex too, on a way that crosses and lies on no other flow, does not keep the vertex, the ring takes it and the flow moves at once; at most one such flow moves to a corner; at a gateway the ring flows back to, flows arriving at its vertex on the ring's side do not keep that vertex, the ring merges with them there, while its side corners stay used by arriving flows; and a vertex of that gateway that faces the target of a flow leaving it is not taken, except where no other port of the target is free: then the ring takes it after all. Where a ring comes closer than 12 px to the border with another lane, its lane grows there, and every lane, symbol and point beyond the border moves away by what it needs. A last pass puts a corner into any piece still slanted, so a flow has right angles only. Then the labels, each with the size `bpmn-js` will draw it in (story 2.20): the page measures it with the viewer's own text renderer, in the document's font, repeating both passes `bpmn-js` makes, the import's box of 90 px for the width and the redraw in that width for the lines, which can take one line more; without a page (the tests in Node), and for a label the renderer cannot measure, the size is estimated (at most 90 px wide, 6.6 px per character, wrapped at blanks). The viewer that measures is the one that then draws the diagram; it is made once the XML is read, so a diagram refused before that makes no host. The places: a flow label is tried right at the exit of a gateway ("ja", "nein"), a flow back's at the gateway's end of its leg, the run along its level, inside the ring first (story 2.25), then in the middle of the flow's longest piece, above or right of it and then on its other side, then the middle of each other piece; the label of an event or a gateway below, above, right and left of it (a gateway's above first), the same farther out, then at its four corners. Each takes the first of its places that no symbol, no piece of a flow and no label placed before it covers; where every place is covered, the one covered least, so a label can still lie on a flow or a symbol in a crowded diagram. A label that then lies in no lane, across the border of its owner's lane with another lane, makes that lane grow there as a ring does, until it lies 4 px inside, and everything beyond the border moves away; every label moves with its owner, the label of an event or a gateway with its symbol, a flow's label with the point of its flow nearest to it, so it keeps its distance to what it names, and every label is checked again after a growth until none crosses a border, so the order of the labels does not change the result (`labelRoom()`, story 2.21; the owner's lane, the lane that holds the symbol or that point). Last, the outer lanes and the pool grow until every waypoint and every label lies inside them, 12 px from the edge: the top lane up, the bottom lane down, every lane and the pool left and right.
5. `appendDiagram()` writes the result as one `bpmndi:BPMNDiagram`, which declares the `bpmndi`, `dc` and `di` namespaces itself, before the last closing `definitions` tag, whatever its prefix; a closing tag in a comment does not count. Everything else of the author's XML stays as written, whatever follows that tag included; the diagram's ids are made unique against every id of the XML. The laid-out XML is kept on the diagram object (`diagram.xml`); the download `.bpmn` gives it (story 2.10). `bpmn-js` is told to open the inserted diagram by its id, since it opens the first one: an author's empty diagram part (a `BPMNPlane` without shapes) stays as written, and is not what is drawn.

`bpmn-js` then draws that XML exactly as it draws XML that came with coordinates.

**The convention** is the one of BPMN with coordinates: a fenced block with the language `bpmn` and BPMN 2.0 XML in it, the diagram part simply left out. dokufix adds no syntax. A renderer that is not dokufix, GitHub for one, shows the block as code, the XML as written. A modeler that draws only what the diagram part places (`bpmn-js` itself says "no diagram to display") needs the laid-out XML, which the download `.bpmn` gives (story 2.10).

- *What is refused,* in this order, each as the detail of the BPMN warning: XML the browser's parser cannot read, or that is no BPMN `definitions`, goes to `bpmn-js` as it is, so its message is the reason, as before; then "Die Bibliothek Mermaid wurde nicht geladen; sie ordnet BPMN ohne Koordinaten an.", "Mehrere Pools lassen sich ohne Koordinaten noch nicht anordnen." (two participants, or two processes), "Das BPMN-XML enthält kein Element, das sich anordnen lässt." (no flow node, or a pool without a process of its own), "Diese Elemente liegen in keiner Bahn: A, B." (lanes exist, and these flow nodes are in none).
- *What is left out, and the rest drawn* (Ben, 2026-10-03): boundary events, text annotations, data objects, data object and store references, associations, data associations, groups, message flows, the content of a sub-process, transaction or ad-hoc sub-process (which is drawn as one symbol), the outer lane of nested lanes (the inner ones are drawn), a lane without an id (its row is laid out), a participant without an id (no pool is drawn), a flow from a node to itself (Mermaid's swimlane layout fails on one), and every sequence flow that touches one of them. Each is a line "BPMN layout, left out: …" on the console, as the import warnings of `bpmn-js` are; the document shows no warning for it.
- *The limits of Mermaid's arrangement* (AC8). All nodes of a lane are placed in one row, so parallel branches belong in different lanes; in one lane they run one after the other and their flows cross or run around each other. A flow back to another lane keeps Mermaid's route, which may run around the whole pool; only a flow back within one row is routed around its row. A loop's step takes a column of its own beside its gateway, not a place above or below it. Two flows back into the same corner of a gateway share the last stub into it, as a merge. A flow back out of a gateway whose four vertices are all used shares a vertex with another flow (review input `r15`, its gateway `gb`), and so do the ends at a parallel gateway with five of them (`r16`, `p1`): there is no free vertex to dock on. A gateway that fans out to many targets can tangle. Only one pool is laid out until story 2.12. A process without lanes is one row. An id that is no XML name, such as `1`, is laid out, but `bpmn-js` does not draw that element and the flows that touch it (an import warning on the console).
- *What the drawing is checked for.* The comparison run measures every laid-out diagram on the SVG `bpmn-js` drew, in both browsers and all four variants: the pool, the lanes, the symbols and the flows the process has, and a clean drawing (AC2): every flow starts and ends on the outline of its symbols, no flow runs through a symbol it does not belong to, no two flow labels overlap, and flows that leave a gateway at one point, in one direction, each have a label of their own; and, since story 2.20, no piece of a flow runs along the outline of a symbol (parallel to a side of its box, less than 3 px off it, beside it for more than 3 px; for a circle or a diamond across the middle of a side), and no label of an event or a gateway, the box of its text as drawn, lies on a piece of a flow or on a symbol, its own included. The reference document has, besides, a process with two loops ("Zwei Schleifen", a pool, 3 lanes, 14 symbols, 15 flows) and four loop cases: a flow back over three tasks, two nested flows back on one side, a flow back in the middle of three lanes, a flow back between two intermediate events; the demo text a loop process and two of them as special cases. On the reference input, `tests/referenz.md`, "Übergabe an die Tourenplanung" (the spike's `diagramme/uebergabe-ohne-koordinaten.bpmn`): pool, 4 lanes, 16 symbols, 17 flows, all four properties hold, in Chromium 153 and Firefox 153 (2026-10-03). The label of the end event "Abwesenheit, nichts zu tun", which lay on a flow in the spike, now stands above its symbol.
- *The net in Node* (story 2.24). `tests/fixtures/bpmn-layout/` keeps 36 inputs: the 13 laid-out diagrams of the reference document, the demo text and the showcase, the large example of the loop analysis (`hund2`), and the 22 inputs of story 2.20's independent review (`r01`–`r22`). Each is five plain files: the author's XML (`<n>.bpmn`), Mermaid's raw positions (`<n>.raw.json`), the label sizes the page measured (`<n>.sizes.json`, `{}` where nothing is measured) and the laid-out XML expected with those sizes and with the estimate (`<n>.measured.bpmn`, `<n>.estimated.bpmn`); `index.json` names the Mermaid version and where each input comes from. `tests/bpmn-fixtures.test.mjs` lays each out again in Node, `readProcess()` → `layoutGeometry()` → `appendDiagram()`, in both modes, and compares byte for byte; a difference names the input, the element and the first line that differs. `tests/bpmn-rules.test.mjs` checks the rules a laid-out diagram keeps on every measured fixture (right angles, ends on the outline, no piece through or along a foreign symbol, no port used by a flow in and a flow out, no runs closer than 12 px but two flows that share the whole rest of their way into one vertex of a gateway from where at most one of them turns, a merge (a T, not a ⊤; a split is none), no small steps, every symbol in its lane, labels inside their lane and the pool and off flows, symbols and labels; `tests/bpmn-rules.mjs` lists them) against `known-breaks.json`, what breaks them today (51 breaks in 13 inputs after story 2.24, 37 in 10 after story 2.25, 15 in 7 after story 2.21): a new break fails, and so does a listed one that is gone. After an intended change of the layout, `npm run fixtures -- --write` writes the expected XML and the known list anew, and `git diff` shows each change; `npm run fixtures` alone names what differs. `npm run capture -- <author.bpmn>…` takes the raw positions and the sizes of an input from `dist/dokufix.html` in Chromium (its fonts), with `mermaidPositions()` and `labelMeasurer()` of `src/app/bpmn.js` bundled into the page, and writes `<n>.raw.json` and `<n>.sizes.json` beside it (`--out <dir>` elsewhere); an input that already has a diagram part (a `BPMNDiagram`, such as `<n>.measured.bpmn`) is skipped with a line naming it, so `tests/fixtures/bpmn-layout/*.bpmn` captures the 36 author XMLs alone: for a new input, and for a new Mermaid version, which the fixture test asks for. It gives the 36 inputs' files byte for byte. In the layout module the routing of flows back has seams of its own since then: the ends at a symbol are read through `portEnds()`, by `fanOut()`, `spreadPorts()` and `loopBack()`, and `portCandidates()`, `conflictScore()` and `growLane()` are functions of their own; the distances of 12 px are named constants by what each is for, seven then and eight since story 2.21, which took `STUB` out of `ATTACH_CLEARANCE`, beside `LABEL_GAP` of 4 px.
- *Three corrections* (story 2.25, from the second analysis of the layout, `_bmad-output/initiative-dokufix/analysis-bpmn-loops/voranalyse-2-21/`, files 4 to 6). A point on one line with its neighbours but outside them, a way out and back, goes before a flow docks (`unfold()`): Mermaid started a flow of the large example with 20 px on the far side of its gateway, and the flow docked there and ran through the diamond. The label of a flow back from a gateway starts at the gateway's end of its leg, inside the ring first, not beside its stub: there it took the corner the gateway's own label needs, and in the reference case of two flows back "Gefunden?" read as one line with "nein". An end Mermaid placed closer than 12 px (`NEAR`) to the middle of a task's side docks at the middle: the offset gave steps of 1 to 6 px and runs 2 to 5 px beside another flow's. Left as they are: in review input `r16` two runs of different flows 8 px apart and two arrivals at the gateway `p2` 3 px apart, which a pass that moves foreign runs apart or joins arrivals into one stem would take, kept for the experiment of entry 22, and labels of gateways with no room in a dense row (the large example).
- *Leftover details* (story 2.21, investigation with copies of the module in `_bmad-output/initiative-dokufix/analysis-bpmn-loops/voranalyse-2-21/skripte/inv221/`). The level of a twin keeps off every symbol of its row within the span: in `r21` the second of two flows between two gateways ran 5 px under the task between them. A flow back out of a gateway whose vertex another flow uses leaves by a side corner (above), and an arrow that arrives at a vertex another flow leaves by joins another arriving flow or docks on a free one (`dockApart()`): in `r19` the flow back and a flow forward left by one vertex, in `r20` an arrow in and an arrow out met at one vertex, in `r14` and `r22` two flows back left by one top vertex. In the large example `Flow_53` arrived at the right vertex of `GW_Merge_Probe`, which `Flow_47` leaves by, with the top vertex used by `Flow_46` and the bottom one only across `Flow_47`: it joins `Flow_46`'s last run into the top vertex. The rule check reads two flows that share the whole rest of their way into one docking point of a gateway, from where at most one of them turns, as a merge, not as a break; so the merges of `ref3`, `r05`, `r16`'s `p2` and two in the large example leave the list. In the large example `Flow_32`, a flow back, merges with `Flow_31` at the top of `GW_Merge_Zitze`, which arriving flows alone use. The label of a ring stays on its level also where the level is shorter than the piece up to it. The lane of a label's owner grows for the label across its border (`labelRoom()`): in `r15`, `r18`, `r22` and the large example labels of flows back and of a gateway stood across the border of a middle lane. 22 listed breaks gone, none new against story 2.25. Left as they are: `r15`'s `gb` and `r16`'s `p1` (above, no free vertex), the side a flow back takes, which counts the crossings of its own ring but not those of the flows it displaces (in `r03` and `r18` weights that chose it otherwise made the picture worse), passes that move foreign runs apart and labels of dense rows in the large example: entry 22.

**What a laid-out diagram costs** (2026-10-03, Chromium 153, `tests/vergleich.mjs` on a document of one heading and a paragraph, `tests/out/28-groesse-ohne.md`, and the same with the reference input, 4 056 B of XML without coordinates, `tests/out/28-groesse-mit.md`; built file of `74ce752`, runs `tests/out/28-v3-groesse-ohne` and `-mit`):

| Variant | Without the diagram | With it | Difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Mit Editor` | 213 299 B | 216 019 B | +2 720 B, the XML in the gzipped source |
| `nur-lesen` | 30 190 B | 71 314 B | +41 124 B, the SVG and the figure |
| `schlank` | 30 193 B | 36 659 B | +6 466 B, the gzipped SVG, the credit and the decoder |
| `kompakt` | 31 082 B | 36 702 B | +5 620 B |

The file itself carries no layout: the laid-out XML exists in the running page only, and a saved file holds the author's XML. The layout module is about 28 600 B of the built script, minified (22 800 B before story 2.21, whose docking apart and joining, side corners and lane growth for labels added 5 800 B; 18 800 B before story 2.20, whose flows back and measured labels added about 3 500 B), most of it the spike's corrections.

**What story 2.8 did to documents** (2026-10-03, `tests/vergleich.mjs --strict`, the built file of `main` at `9dbb40d` against a build of the new sources, both with the demo text as it was before the story, Chromium 153 and Firefox 153): all 34 screenshots per browser are pixel-identical, and every assertion is green. A document without BPMN that lacks coordinates is drawn as before: Mermaid is asked only for a layout. The built file grows by 29 033 B (208 491 → 237 524 B, `74ce752`): 20 256 B of script (the layout module, its browser part in `bpmn.js`, the guards for a page without Mermaid) and 8 777 B the demo text's new section and special cases, four laid-out diagrams.

**Colours.** The viewer is told to draw with three custom properties instead of colours (`BPMN_VIEWER_CONFIG`): `var(--dokufix-bpmn-fill)`, `-stroke` and `-label`. So the SVG names no colour, and `src/doc.css` defines the properties on the figure and changes them per class: the document's greys for lines, pools and lanes (`#3a3a3f`, `#8e8e92`, a pool `#fafafa`), the link colour `#0066cc` for tasks, call activities, gateways and the events that do not end a process, `#1c1c1e` for end events and labels, `#6e6e73` for message flows. A later colour scheme needs no second rendering. Only the light scheme exists (decision of 2026-10-02). No `bpmn-js` CSS and no font file is loaded.

**Labels and fonts.** The viewer's text renderer uses the document's system font stack at 12 px (`BPMN_FONT`), and it measures the labels with the font of the author's machine when it draws. A reader with another font sees the finished picture. The comparison run checks that in Chromium (AC7): it shows the `nur-lesen` export with the font as drawn and with two other installed system fonts, and measures every label against what holds it, a label inside a task, a call activity, a pool or a lane against that shape, any other against the picture. Measured 2026-10-03 on the reference document, drawn with Segoe UI: as drawn, and with Noto Sans, all 32 labels fit; with DejaVu Sans, which is wider, 3 of 32 do not: "Statistik zählen" runs out of its task by 1.5 px, and "Nachricht" and "Sortiert", at the right edge of their picture, are cut off by 1.3 and 0.7 px. On the demo text, 23 labels: as drawn and with Noto Sans all fit; with DejaVu Sans "Feierabend", at the right edge of the special case, is cut off by 3 px. The run takes one picture per diagram with a label that does not fit, named by the font and the diagram's place among the BPMN diagrams, and lists it with each label in its note and in `schrift.json`. The pictures of the run of 2026-10-03 on the current sources: `tests/out/27-schrift/chromium/exports/schrift-dejavu-sans-1.png` ("Statistik zählen", "Nachricht"), `schrift-dejavu-sans-2.png` there ("Sortiert"), and `tests/out/27-schrift-demo/chromium/exports/schrift-dejavu-sans-2.png` ("Feierabend"); the cases are flagged for the product owner. The check notes what it finds and fails only when a label does not fit as drawn. Since story 2.8 it also measures whether a label outside its symbol (an event's, a gateway's, a flow's) lies on a flow other than its own (AC9 of 2.8), and notes that with a picture too. Measured 2026-10-03 on the built file of `74ce752` (`tests/out/28-v3/`), drawn with Segoe UI: as drawn and with Noto Sans every label fits and none lies on a flow, in the reference document (61 labels) and in the demo text (57). With DejaVu Sans, besides the four cases of story 2.7, the laid-out diagrams have four labels that do not fit: in "Übergabe an die Tourenplanung" the task labels "Abmeldung sofort eintragen" and "Erinnerungen enden automatisch" run out of their tasks by 1.3 and 2.8 px; "Wieder ausleihbar", at the right edge of the picture of "Ohne Bahnen" (reference document and demo text), is cut off by 2.8 px; in the demo text "Fernleihe bestellen" runs out of its task by 3.8 px. And with DejaVu Sans one label of story 2.7's diagram "Wöchentliches Sortieren", the gateway's "Was ist zu tun?", lies on the flow G2. Pictures: `tests/out/28-v3/referenz/chromium/exports/schrift-dejavu-sans-<n>.png` and `tests/out/28-v3/demo/chromium/exports/schrift-dejavu-sans-<n>.png`; the cases are flagged for the product owner. A task label is wrapped by `bpmn-js` to the width of its task in the author's font, so a wider task does not help; it overflows in a wider font whatever the layout does.

**Attribution.** "Gezeichnet mit bpmn-js", where only "bpmn-js" is a link to https://bpmn.io, stands below every BPMN diagram in every variant (decisions of 2026-10-01 and 2026-10-03; it read "gerendert mit bpmn.io" first, which sounded as if data went to an outside service): a `<figcaption>` outside the SVG container, 12 px in `#6e6e73`. In `schlank` it is readable text beside the gzipped SVG, and it is there with scripts off. The library is used unmodified, and the exports carry only the output of its export function (D5, 2026-10-01).

**The licence position** (story 2.10, AC6). The bpmn.io licence of `bpmn-js` is the MIT licence with one more condition: the code that shows the bpmn.io watermark must not be removed or changed, and where the software is used in a website or application, the watermark stays fully visible (see *Licence information*). How dokufix uses the library:

- *Where it runs.* In the editor, `dist/dokufix.html`, and in every `Mit Editor` file, loaded from jsDelivr as it is published (`bpmn-navigated-viewer.production.min.js` of 18.31.0), unmodified. It draws each BPMN diagram into a transient host of its own, `position:fixed` 10 000 px left of the window and `aria-hidden`, which is destroyed after each diagram (`src/app/bpmn.js`); and it runs as the live viewer in the large view of a BPMN diagram, in a transient container, destroyed when the view closes (story 2.11). It runs in no read-only export: `nur-lesen`, `schlank` and `kompakt` carry no tag, no URL and no code of it, which the comparison run and the failure-case run check in every file.
- *Where its logo is visible.* In the live viewer of story 2.11: wherever the library shows a diagram to the reader, in the large view of a BPMN diagram in the editor and in `Mit Editor`, its logo stands where the library puts it, bottom right, unmodified and fully visible; nothing of dokufix covers it (`tests/vergleich.mjs` checks it by `elementFromPoint` in both browsers), and a click on it opens the library's own lightbox. Where the library only draws for the column, into the off-screen host, the logo is in that host and `saveSVG()` does not export it.
- *What a file carries of it.* Only the output of its export function: the SVG of `saveSVG()`, finished by `finishBpmnSvg()` (hit areas out, ids prefixed, the size of the column), in all four variants. The downloaded picture `.svg` is that SVG, its custom properties resolved and its size set. The downloaded `.bpmn` is no output of the library: the author's XML, or the author's XML with the diagram part dokufix laid out.
- *The attribution.* "Gezeichnet mit bpmn-js", the name a link to bpmn.io, below every BPMN diagram, in all four variants; story 2.10 puts its line above it and leaves its markup as it was.
- *The product owner's assessment* (decision D5, 2026-10-01): this use is consistent with the licence and defensible; bpmn.io is not asked to confirm. It rests on the unmodified library, the drawing off-screen, files that carry only the output of the export function, the attribution below every diagram, and the logo in the live viewer of story 2.11, which is there since that story.

**What it costs** (2026-10-03, Chromium 153, `tests/vergleich.mjs` on a document of one heading and a paragraph, `tests/out/27-groesse-ohne.md`, and the same with the BPMN diagram of the demo text, 7 736 B of XML, `tests/out/27-groesse-mit.md`):

| Variant | Without the diagram | With it | Difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Mit Editor` | 183 929 B | 187 937 B | +4 008 B, the XML in the gzipped source |
| `nur-lesen` | 30 190 B | 60 677 B | +30 487 B, the SVG and the figure |
| `schlank` | 30 193 B | 35 773 B | +5 580 B, the gzipped SVG, the credit and the decoder |
| `kompakt` | 31 082 B | 35 818 B | +4 736 B |

The SVG of `bpmn-js` writes every style on every element. In the `nur-lesen` file of the reference document the figures of its two BPMN diagrams with coordinates are 47 039 and 18 974 B, those of its two Mermaid diagrams 59 766 and 27 272 B. The library itself is 194 602 B (54 624 B gzipped), loaded from the CDN and carried in no file.

**What story 2.7 did to documents** (2026-10-03, `tests/vergleich.mjs --strict`, the built file before the story, `b04729d`, against the one after each step, the reference document and the demo text as they were before the story, Chromium 153 and Firefox 153, 136 screenshots per step):

- *Step A, the figure, alone* (commit `fd56a27`): all 136 screenshots are pixel-identical. Every assertion is green. What the screenshots do not show, and what changed:

### Footnotes

The heading `marked-footnote` puts above the footnotes is visually hidden, but it is a heading: it shows in the table of contents and in the rail. Its text is set to "Fußnoten" through the library's `description` option (`src/app.js`); the default is the English "Footnotes".

Standard GFM footnotes work: `[^id]` places a marker, `[^id]:` defines it, and `marked-footnote` (registered via the single `marked.use()` call in the file) renders the definition list at the document end with return arrows. dokufix adds no syntax here.

**Hover / focus preview.** Each marker carries a preview of its footnote, revealed on `:hover` or `:focus-within` of the host `<sup class="dokufix-fn-host">`. The reveal is **pure CSS** — there is no listener — which is what lets it work in the JS-free `nur-lesen` export. `attachFootnotePreviews()` only injects inert markup at render time; because every read-only export renders and then copies the preview (see *Render passes*), and a `Mit Editor` file renders again when it is opened, the previews travel into all four downloads without any export-specific code.

**Preview content is flattened to inline.** This is load-bearing, not cosmetic. The preview `<span>` lives in the `<sup>` that sits inside the paragraph carrying the marker. A footnote definition is block content (`<p>`, sometimes lists) — and a `<p>` nested inside a `<p>` makes the HTML parser close the outer paragraph early. In the live DOM you would not notice; in an *export*, where the markup is serialised and re-parsed by the recipient's browser, it would quietly shred the document structure. So `fnFlattenInline()` unwraps block elements (joining them with a space) and keeps only phrasing content. Links are unwrapped to their text as well, for a second reason: the preview is `aria-hidden="true"` (the footnote text is already reachable through the marker's own link, and announcing it twice is noise), and an `aria-hidden` subtree must not contain focusable elements. Backrefs and nested markers are stripped, the latter so a footnote citing a footnote cannot nest previews.

**Positioning, and the trade-off it carries.** A centred box cannot fit when its marker sits closer to the viewport edge than half the box width — at ~900 px (where the content column nearly fills the window) a right-edge marker pushed the preview ~200 px off-screen. Plain CSS cannot detect that without JavaScript.

CSS anchor positioning fixes it: `@position-try` keeps the box on screen at every tested width (1600/1200/900/800/600/390), each preview anchored to its own marker. The fallback chain covers **both axes** — `block-start` tries for the inline overflow, `block-end` tries for the block axis. An earlier version listed only `block-start` tries, which vary the inline axis alone, so a marker near the top of the viewport had nothing to flip to; the block axis had never been tested (the widths above are six numbers and no heights). The box also carries a `max-height` with a fade: a preview is a preview, and without a ceiling a long footnote grows past the viewport top, where content is unreachable because browsers do not make overflow above the scroll origin scrollable.

Three non-obvious requirements, all load-bearing:

- the block must reach **every** variant. For a while it did not: it sat in the app stylesheet twice and in the export stylesheet not at all, so the read-only exports, the artifacts that actually travel, kept the centred base and kept clipping. Since story 2.1 there is one copy, today in `src/doc.css`, and the exports embed the block built from it (see *Document styles*). `node tests/check-doc-styles.mjs` fails when a rule for it turns up anywhere else;
- the host `<sup>` must **not** be `position:relative` inside the `@supports` block — a tiny containing block leaves the try-fallbacks nothing to evaluate against and they silently never fire;
- the `transition` must stay — without it Firefox does not reveal the preview at all.

**In the editor.** In edit mode the preview scrolls inside its pane, and a preview with an unpositioned host is placed against the nearest positioned ancestor, which was the page. The hidden previews of a long document then made the page behind the editor thousands of pixels high (5 410 against 900 px with the demo text in Chromium 153; with the reference document 8 394 against 1 000 px there and 8 415 in Firefox 153), and a click on an entry of the table of contents in the preview scrolled the page as well as the pane: the toolbar left the window by 100 px, and the wheel did not bring it back. One rule in `src/app.css` ends that: in edit mode `.layout`, the box below the toolbar, is `position:relative` with `overflow:clip` and `min-height:0`. It is the containing block of the previews and cuts off what reaches past it, so the page has nothing to scroll. `overflow:clip` and not `hidden`, because `scrollIntoView()` scrolls a box with `overflow:hidden` too, and did, by the height of the pane's header. Where a preview appears does not change by this, with one exception: it stands at its marker and may reach out of the preview pane over the source, as before (accepted by Ben, 2026-10-02); a preview that stood above its marker over the toolbar now stands below its marker, whole (measured with markers 24 px below the pane's top edge, both browsers: no preview is cut off). Read mode and the exports are untouched: there no ancestor of a preview is positioned. Not taken: a scrolling element around the preview with the document positioned inside it, which keeps a preview inside the pane and lets a pane narrower than a preview scroll sideways.

**In a table cell.** Every table stands in a wrapper that scrolls sideways (see *Tables*), and a box that scrolls cuts off whatever is placed inside it. The preview of a footnote cited in a cell is not placed inside it: the host is not positioned in the `@supports` block, and neither is the wrapper, so the preview's containing block lies outside the wrapper, and it reaches out of the table uncut. Measured in Chromium 153 and Playwright's Firefox 153 at 900 px before the story, with every table in an unpositioned `div` with `overflow-x:auto`: the preview stands exactly where it stood without the wrapper. The comparison run holds that place in Chromium (see *Comparison run*). The wrapper must therefore never be positioned, nor carry anything else that makes a containing block (`transform`, `filter`, `contain`, `container-type`, `will-change`); `tests/facets.test.mjs` fails when a rule of it does.
One case is cut off: an engine without anchor positioning (Firefox before 147, Safari before 26). There the host is `position:relative`, the base rule, and the preview's containing block is its `<sup>`, inside the wrapper: the preview is cut at the wrapper's edges, and one that reaches past the right edge makes the wrapper scrollable even while the preview is hidden (measured in Chromium with the `@supports` block switched off: `scrollWidth` 903 against 836). Without a wrapper the same preview was cut by the window and widened the page. Recorded as a known limit and deferred (`_bmad-output/initiative-dokufix/deferred-work.md`).

**The `@supports` gate is syntactic, not behavioural.** `@supports (anchor-name:--a) and (position-try-fallbacks:--b)` asks whether the engine *parses* these properties, not whether the fallbacks actually fire — and Firefox answers yes to the first while failing the second. So the gate cannot separate a working engine from a partial one, and any engine that parses the syntax gets the refinement whether or not it can honour it. This is why positioning is verified by **measurement** rather than by trusting the gate.

**Engine behaviour.** Chromium/Edge reveal in ~40–100 ms at every width. Firefox was initially reported here as slow (~700 ms) and as leaving previews stuck open — **both were artifacts of Playwright's synthetic mouse and do not reproduce for a human.** Confirmed by hands-on review (2026-07-16): with a real mouse in Firefox the preview appears immediately.

One real behaviour worth knowing: **clicking** a marker leaves its preview open, because the click focuses the anchor and the reveal rule is `:hover, :focus-within`. Clicking anywhere else in the document dismisses it. That is inherent to the JS-free design rather than a defect — `:focus-within` is exactly what makes the preview keyboard-reachable, and on touch devices (where `:hover` does not exist) it is the only way a preview can be seen at all. Accepted by the product owner after review.

Automated coverage of this feature is therefore only as good as synthetic input allows; the harness assertions around un-hover in Firefox are marked as known synthetic-input deviations rather than treated as product failures. Judge hover behaviour with a real pointer.

**Landing highlight and return-path disambiguation.** `marked-footnote` renders every reference to the same footnote with the *same visible number* and the *same href*: three citations of `[^norm]` all read `1` and all link to `#footnote-norm`, while the definition grows three return arrows `↩ ↩² ↩³`. On arrival you cannot tell which entry you landed on, nor which arrow leads back to where you came from.

`:target` only ever knows the current fragment, so CSS cannot distinguish the three cases. The only JS-free fix is to give each marker a **distinct** target: `linkFootnoteReturnPaths()` gives every arrow a stable id (`footnote-back-<id>[-N]`) and points the matching marker at it. Then

- `a[data-footnote-backref]:target` marks exactly the arrow that leads back, and
- `li:has(a[data-footnote-backref]:target)` shades the definition around it, while
- `li:target` still shades when arriving via the plain `#footnote-<id>` anchor (external bookmarks, copied URLs).

All pure CSS, so it works in the JS-free export. `attachFootnotePreviews()` must run **before** the retarget, which is its place in `DOCUMENT_PASSES` — it resolves each definition through the marker's href, so retargeting first would build every preview out of the `↩` anchor instead of the footnote. That ordering used to be guarded by a comment alone, with a silent failure mode (every preview rendering as `↩`, no error); the lookup is now scoped `li#…`, so a wrong order resolves to nothing and skips the preview instead of producing a confidently wrong one.

**Pairing goes through the definition, not the marker's id.** The obvious implementation — read the arrow's `href`, `querySelector` that id, retarget it — is wrong, because `marked-footnote` does not guarantee those ids are unique. A document containing both `[^bgb]` (cited twice) and `[^bgb-2]` mints `id="footnote-ref-bgb-2"` **twice**; `querySelector` takes the first, and `[^bgb-2]`'s marker gets repointed at footnote `bgb`'s arrow — the reader clicks and lands on the wrong footnote, while the hover preview still shows the right text. A marker's *href* names its definition unambiguously, so each definition pairs its Nth marker with its Nth arrow; both are in reference order. Ids generated this way share one flat namespace with the definitions (`footnote-<label>`), so `[^back-x]` beside `[^x]` can still collide — on a collision the marker keeps pointing at its definition, which keeps the jump correct and loses only the arrow marking for that one reference.

**Accessibility trade-off — the cost of doing this without JavaScript.** The marker now lands on the return arrow, which sits at the *end* of the footnote text. Measured on a real export: `:target` resolves to `<a id="footnote-back-norm-2" aria-label="Back to reference norm">`, so a screen-reader user activating a footnote marker hears *"Back to reference"* before the footnote's prose, and must navigate backwards to read it. It is shipped knowingly, not overlooked.

Two costs that a first write-up of this trade-off missed, and that anyone weighing the reversal should have in front of them:

- **Sighted readers are not unaffected.** Fragment navigation start-aligns the target, and the target is now the arrow at the *end* of the definition. A footnote taller than the 90 px `scroll-margin-top` therefore lands the reader on `↩` with its own prose scrolled above the viewport top — they must scroll **up** to read what they came for. The `li`'s `scroll-margin-top` cannot compensate, because the `li` is no longer the target. Short footnotes are unaffected; long ones are exactly the case policy and audit documents produce.
- **Assistive-technology users pay the cost and get none of the benefit.** All arrows of a multi-referenced footnote carry the same accessible name — `[^bgb]`'s two arrows both read `aria-label="Back to reference bgb"`. Telling the arrows apart is the entire point of the feature, and the only signal that distinguishes them is colour. So the group that pays the reading-order cost is the one group the feature cannot help.

*Reversal (≈15 lines):* drop `linkFootnoteReturnPaths()` and the `:has()` selector, keep `li:target`. The landing highlight survives intact and JS-free; only "which arrow is mine" is lost. *Heavier alternative:* one empty landing anchor per reference at the **start** of each definition, paired to its arrow through a bounded set of static rules (`li:has(.dokufix-fn-landing-2:target) .dokufix-fn-back-2`), which restores reading order at the cost of N rule pairs in the document styles. Tracked in `_bmad-output/initiative-dokufix/deferred-work.md`.

**Size cost.** The preview duplicates each footnote's text inline, roughly doubling it. Measured 2026-07-16 on a document with three short footnotes (two definitions, one cited twice), all four variants through the same build and the same document, `b74cfce` (frontmatter panel merged, previews not yet added) as the baseline:

| variant | before | after | delta |
|---|---|---|---|
| `nur-lesen` | 8 838 B | 12 045 B | +3 207 B (+36.3 %) |
| `schlank` | 8 840 B | 12 047 B | +3 207 B (+36.3 %) |
| `kompakt` | 8 705 B | 11 268 B | +2 563 B (+29.4 %) |
| `Mit Editor` | 113 056 B | 136 431 B | +23 375 B (+20.7 %) |

Read these carefully, because they are not the numbers an earlier draft carried. They span **story 1.2 and story 1.3 and the review patches**, not the preview alone, and they are larger than the first figures partly because the read-only exports now actually carry the `@supports` block they were always meant to have — the earlier measurement was taken on exports that silently lacked it. `Mit Editor` is a different animal again: it embeds the whole application, so its delta is mostly the source growth of the branch, not the cost of the feature.

gzip absorbs much of the duplication, which is exactly the kind of redundancy it is good at. Footnote-heavy documents pay proportionally more; the per-footnote marginal cost is about the length of the footnote's own text.

### Frontmatter (YAML / JSON metadata header)

A metadata block at the very top of the document renders as a collapsible panel above the first heading, not as document content.

Without this, marked treats the block as ordinary Markdown: per CommonMark a lone `---` is a thematic break, *except* when it follows paragraph text, where it becomes a setext H2 underline. So `---\ntitle: Foo\n---` rendered as `<hr>` plus `<h2>title: Foo</h2>` — visible garbage in the preview and in every export.

**Delimiters.** The first line must be exactly `---` (sniffed as JSON when the content starts with `{`, YAML otherwise), or explicitly `---json` / `---yaml`. A closing `---` line must follow. TOML (`+++`) is not supported.

**Panel.** `<details class="dokufix-frontmatter">`, collapsed by default — the document should read as a document on first open. The summary line shows a digest of whichever of `title`, `version`, `date`, `author` are present (case-insensitive), in that order, joined with `·` — all of them, not just the first — falling back to an entry count when none is. Expanding reveals every key/value pair; nested maps become indented sub-rows, sequences become lists. Everything is HTML-escaped. Because it's a native `<details>`, it is keyboard-operable and needs no JavaScript — it works in the `nur-lesen` export. The search of the reading view finds its keys and values, not its summary (epic 5, story 8; see *Search*, *Hits in metadata and code*): a click on its result opens a closed panel and brings the hit into view, and the panel stays open as if the reader had opened it. Saved and exported files are not changed by it.

**The YAML subset — and its limits.** dokufix does *not* bundle a YAML library. One (~30 KB) against a ~16 KB artifact fails the "body for information" test. Instead there is a deliberate subset covering what document frontmatter actually contains:

| Supported | Not supported |
|---|---|
| `key: value` pairs | block scalars (`\|`, `>`) |
| nested maps by indentation | anchors / aliases (`&`, `*`) |
| sequences of scalars (`- item`), indented or flush with the key | sequences of maps (`- key: value`) |
| single- and double-quoted scalars | tags (`!!str`) |
| `#` comments, full-line and trailing | flow collections (`{a: 1}`, `[a, b]`) — except as the JSON path |
| | tab indentation, multi-document streams |

Anything outside the subset makes the parser fail **loudly rather than partially**: the panel then shows the raw block verbatim under a "nicht lesbar" summary. A half-parsed panel that silently dropped a key would be a data-integrity failure; showing the original text is honest and loses nothing. There is deliberately no type coercion — values stay strings, so YAML's `no`-becomes-`false` class of surprises can't occur. The values are displayed, never computed on.

Fail-loudly is a claim worth auditing, because it quietly failed in five places at once and every one of them looked fine from the outside: duplicate keys overwrote each other, `__proto__` disappeared through `Object.prototype`'s setter, `{a: 1}` under an explicit `---yaml` fence half-parsed into key `{a` / value `1}`, `- Autor Name: Ben` slipped past a sequence guard whose alphabet was narrower than the parser's, and an unknown escape like `é` rendered literally. Four of them now throw. The fifth is kept: on a map without a prototype `__proto__` is an entry like any other, and the panel shows it. `tests/frontmatter.test.mjs` holds all five as cases, beside the rest of this section. The lesson generalises: **every regex that recognises a key must use the same alphabet.** Three used to disagree, and each disagreement was a silent bug.

**Multi-document streams** are listed as unsupported above and are worth spelling out, because the failure is not loud: the closing-`---` search stops at the first delimiter, so `---\na: 1\n---\nb: 2\n---` parses document 1 into a confident-looking panel and leaks `b: 2` into the body as a setext `<h2>`. That is standard frontmatter behaviour and is not going to change; it is simply not the raw panel this section otherwise promises.

**Not mistaken for a thematic break, and not swallowing prose.** A document may legitimately open with `---`, so detection is separate from parsing — and it needs **two** signals, not one. "First meaningful line is a `key:`" is not enough on its own, because that shape is equally an ordinary sentence: `---\nNote: this is a draft.\n---` used to be reinterpreted as a one-entry mapping, which moved the author's prose out of the body into a collapsed panel. The tie must never break toward hiding body text. So a block is frontmatter when it holds **two or more entries, or one recognized metadata key** (`title`/`version`/`date`/`author`) — one prose line satisfies neither, while a lone `title: X` still gets its panel. A `{` first line is JSON and unambiguous on its own.

The second way body text used to disappear was an **unterminated** block: the closing-`---` search is document-wide, so it latched onto the next thematic break and dragged whole paragraphs into the candidate, which then failed to parse and buried them under "nicht lesbar". Frontmatter never contains blank-line-separated prose; a document does. That signature now disqualifies the block, so the malformed source renders as what it is and the prose stays where the author put it.

`---\nSome intro.\n---` and an empty `---\n---` are left completely untouched. An explicit but empty header (`---json\n---`) is consumed and renders nothing — announcing "nicht lesbar" over an empty `<pre>` would be a lie. A block that looks like frontmatter but fails to parse gets the raw panel; a block that doesn't look like frontmatter at all is not frontmatter.

**Title derivation.** `deriveDocTitle()` is the single source of truth for "what is this document called?", used by the download filename and all three read-only export `<title>`s. It is handed the source and reads the **body**, i.e. the source with frontmatter split off. Previously each of those four sites ran `/^#\s+(.+?)\s*$/m` against the raw source, so a YAML comment like `# internal draft` won against the document's real `# Heading` — files downloaded as `internal-draft.html`. Splitting the frontmatter off fixes that.

### Callouts

A blockquote whose first line is only an alert marker renders as a callout: a note or a warning that reads as one.

```markdown
> [!NOTE]
> Der Automat druckt den Beleg auf Wunsch ein zweites Mal.
```

| Marker | Label | Symbol (Octicon) | Colour of edge, label and symbol |
|---|---|---|---|
| `[!NOTE]` | Hinweis | `info` | `#0969da` |
| `[!TIP]` | Tipp | `light-bulb` | `#1a7f37` |
| `[!IMPORTANT]` | Wichtig | `report` | `#8250df` |
| `[!WARNING]` | Achtung | `alert` | `#9a6700` |
| `[!CAUTION]` | Vorsicht | `stop` | `#cf222e` |

**The convention is GitHub's:** the alerts of GitHub Flavored Markdown, these five types and no others. dokufix adds no syntax. A callout at the top level of a document renders as an alert on GitHub, with GitHub's English labels, and in a renderer that does not know the convention as a quotation that begins with the line `[!NOTE]`, which is what dokufix itself showed until story 2.2. One thing dokufix does and GitHub does not: GitHub's documentation says that alerts cannot be nested within other elements, and dokufix builds a callout at any depth. A marker in a quote inside a quote, in a list item or in another callout is a callout in dokufix and a plain quotation on GitHub.

**What is recognised.** `buildCallouts()` in `src/app/callouts.js` is the document pass `Hinweise`. It works on the markup `marked` emits, not on the Markdown, and leaves the tokens of `marked` alone.

| Source | Result |
|---|---|
| `> [!NOTE]`, then `> text` | callout; the marker is gone, the content keeps its formatting, lists and nested blocks |
| `> [!note]`, `> [!Note]` | callout: the case does not matter |
| `> [!WARNING]`, then a list, a new paragraph or a code block | callout; no empty paragraph is left where the marker stood |
| `> [!IMPORTANT]` with a hard break behind it (two blanks or a backslash) | callout; the line break is gone |
| `> [!CAUTION]` and nothing else | callout with its label alone |
| a marker in a quote inside a quote, in a list item, in another callout | callout; on GitHub a plain quotation |
| `> [!NOTE] text`, anything else on the marker's line | ordinary blockquote, unchanged; the one exception is a raw `<br>`, see the known limits |
| `> [!FOO]`, an unknown type | ordinary blockquote, unchanged |
| a marker that is not the first line of its quote | ordinary blockquote, unchanged |

**The markup** is `<div class="dokufix-callout dokufix-callout-note" role="note">`, and its first child is `<p class="dokufix-callout-label">Hinweis</p>`. The type is that word, text in the markup; colour and symbol are decoration from `src/doc.css`. A callout is document content like the metadata panel: the pass produces it in the preview, every export copies the preview, so it is in all four variants, and `nur-lesen` needs no script for it.

**Beside the product's warning.** `[!WARNING]` reads "Achtung", so that the word "Warnung" belongs to the product's own warning alone (see *Render passes*). The two differ in more than colour: a callout has an edge on its left and nothing else around it, and its label is a line of its own with a symbol; the warning is a box with a border all round and a background, and "Warnung:" opens its first line.

**A heading inside a callout is no document heading.** `> ### Titel` in a callout is styled as a heading and is nothing else: it gets no id, so a link to it has no target; it is not in the inline table of contents, not in the rail of the editor and not in the rail of an export; with numbering switched on it gets no number, and the headings after it are numbered as they are without it. The first three follow from `documentHeadings()`. The numbering is switched off in `src/doc.css`, by rules for headings inside `.dokufix-callout` that outweigh the numbering rules: where a heading stands is something a selector sees, so no class is set for it. A heading in an ordinary blockquote counts as before.

**The symbols** are Octicons, the ones GitHub shows for the five alert types: `info`, `light-bulb`, `report`, `alert` and `stop`, each in its 16 px form, from the npm package `@primer/octicons` in version 19.38.0 (MIT licence, "Copyright (c) 2026 GitHub Inc."). The package is not a dependency. The path data of `build/svg/<name>-16.svg` stands unchanged in `src/doc.css`, each icon as a `data:` URI on the `::before` of the label, with `<`, `>` and `#` percent-encoded and the colour of the type as `fill`. So a symbol looks the same on every system, travels with the document styles into all four variants, and the markup stays text. The URIs pass through esbuild, the build's guard against `</style`, the style check and `compactCss()` of the export path as they are written: `tests/callouts.test.mjs` compares the built file with the source, and the comparison run loads each symbol in each variant and expects a picture of 16 × 16 px.

**The licence notice** for the icons is in every file since story 2.18: the Octicons are one of the four entries behind the link "license information", with the copyright line above and the text of the MIT licence. Until then `dist/dokufix.html` and every file written from it carried the path data of the five icons and no notice; `src/doc.css` names set, version and licence in a comment, which the build removes. See *Licence information*.

**What callouts did to documents** (2026-10-02, `tests/vergleich.mjs`, the built file before story 2.2 against the one after, Chromium 153 and Firefox 153, with the reference document as it was before the story and with the demo text, neither of which has a callout): all 136 screenshots are pixel-identical, and all assertions are green. Outside its `<style>` each of the twelve read-only exports is the file it was, byte for byte; the stylesheet in it grew by 4 634 B, the rules for callouts. The file itself and every `Mit Editor` file grew by 5 930 B (72 302 → 78 232 B). Then the reference document got its five callouts; with it all assertions are green in all four variants and both browsers, and its sizes are under *Download variants*. Control: built with the two numbering rules for headings inside a callout and the filter in `documentHeadings()` taken out, the run fails in every variant (Chromium) on the three checks that concern them: the heading has an id and stands in table of contents and rail, it counts, and the page with numbering on equals the one with the heading forced into the count.

**Two known limits,** both because the pass sees the markup and not the tokens; telling these cases apart needs an extension of `marked` on token level.

- *A masked marker becomes a callout.* `> \[!NOTE\]` is how Markdown writes a quotation that begins with the literal text `[!NOTE]`. `marked` emits the same markup for it as for `> [!NOTE]`. Until then a quotation that is to begin with that text writes it as code (`` > `[!NOTE]` ``) or puts more on the marker's line (`> [!NOTE] text`); both stay quotations.
- *A raw `<br>` on the marker's line becomes a callout too.* `> [!NOTE]<br>text` has something else on the marker's line and should stay a quotation. It arrives as `<p>[!NOTE]<br>text</p>`, the markup of a marker with a hard break behind it, so it becomes a callout and the `<br>` is removed.

### Status chips

A code span that starts with a colour dot, a blank and a label renders as a status chip: a small coloured label that marks the state of a process or a system, in running text, in a table cell, in a heading.

```markdown
| Automat 1 | Eingang | `🟢 in Betrieb` |
```

| Dot | Class | Word for assistive technology | Label | Mark | Tint | Contrast of label, of mark | Shape of the mark |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 🟢 | `dokufix-chip-green` | grün | `#116329` | `#1a7f37` | `#dafbe1` | 6.64 : 1, 4.56 : 1 | filled circle |
| 🟡 | `dokufix-chip-yellow` | gelb | `#7d4e00` | `#9a6700` | `#fff8c5` | 6.58 : 1, 4.52 : 1 | triangle |
| 🔴 | `dokufix-chip-red` | rot | `#a40e26` | `#cf222e` | `#ffebe9` | 6.86 : 1, 4.67 : 1 | square |
| ⚪ | `dokufix-chip-grey` | grau | `#424a53` | `#57606a` | `#eaeef2` | 7.71 : 1, 5.48 : 1 | ring |
| 🔵 | `dokufix-chip-blue` | blau | `#0550ae` | `#0969da` | `#ddf4ff` | 6.68 : 1, 4.56 : 1 | diamond |

**The convention is a starting point** (decision D2, 2026-10-01): dokufix adds no syntax, and a better one may replace this. What counts as a chip is decided in one module, `src/app/chips.js`; nothing else tests for a colour dot, and `tests/chips.test.mjs` fails when another module of the script starts to. In a renderer that does not know the convention, GitHub among them, the span stays what it is in Markdown: inline code that begins with the emoji, `🟢 Live` in a monospace font. The status is still readable there, by the dot and the label.

**What is recognised.** `buildChips()` is the document pass `Status-Chips`. Like the callout pass it works on the markup `marked` emits, not on the Markdown, and leaves the tokens of `marked` alone.

| Source | Result |
|---|---|
| `` `🟢 Live` ``, `` `🟡 Live` ``, `` `🔴 Live` ``, `` `⚪ Live` ``, `` `🔵 Live` `` | chip with the class of its colour; the dot is gone, the label stays |
| the dot with a variation selector behind it (U+FE0F, as an emoji keyboard writes ⚪) | chip |
| `` `🟢  Live` ``, more blanks behind the dot; blanks behind the label | chip; they do not belong to the label |
| in running text, a table cell, a heading, a link, bold or emphasised text, a list item, a quotation, a callout, a footnote | chip; in a link it is underlined |
| `` `x` ``, `` `x 🟢` `` | ordinary code, unchanged |
| `` `🟢` ``, `` `🟢x` ``: no label, or no blank | ordinary code, unchanged |
| a tab or a no-break space in place of the blank; a dot that is none of the five (🟣, 🟠, ⚫) | ordinary code, unchanged |
| a line `🟢 Live` in a code block, fenced or indented | unchanged |
| `<code>` written as HTML that holds markup, `<code>🟢 <b>Live</b></code>` | unchanged |

**The markup** is `<span class="dokufix-chip dokufix-chip-green"><span class="dokufix-chip-status">grün: </span>Live</span>`. A chip is document content like a callout: the pass produces it in the preview, every export copies the preview, so it is in all four variants, and `nur-lesen` needs no script for it. In a footnote it is a chip in the footnote's preview as well, because the preview keeps `<span>` elements.

**The look** is in `src/doc.css`: a pill in small capitals of the monospace stack the document already uses, 11 px, on a light tint. The mark has a colour of the callouts' palette (grey has none there); the label is a darker shade of it, so that it reads well at that size (Ben, 2026-10-02: the text a little darker). The contrast against the tint is at least 6.5 : 1 for the label and at least 4.5 : 1 for the mark; `tests/chips.test.mjs` works both out from the rules. A chip does not make its line higher: measured in a table row, a heading and a callout, in Chromium 153 and Firefox 153, the block is as high with the chip as with plain text in its place. A chip in a link carries an underline of its own: the link's underline does not reach a box that sets its own colour, and without one nothing would say that the chip leads somewhere. In print the tint and the mark stay (`print-color-adjust`), as the symbols of the callouts do: an export printed to PDF from Chromium 153 without background graphics shows both.

**Not by colour alone.** Two things say the status besides the colour.

- *A mark before the label, a different shape per colour:* a filled circle, a triangle, a square, a ring, a diamond. They are drawn in the document styles, the `::before` of the chip, with no font and no image. The spike gave ⚪ and 🔵 one look; here the five differ pairwise. The comparison run photographs them all in one colour and expects every pair to differ. Where the system replaces the colours (`forced-colors`), backgrounds are dropped; the marks are kept by a rule of their own, and the chip's border, transparent otherwise, outlines it (looked at in Chromium 153 with forced colours emulated; no check holds it).
- *The name of the colour as text:* the inner `<span class="dokufix-chip-status">` holds "grün: ", visually hidden and not `display:none`, so a screen reader says "grün: Live" and two chips with the same label and different colours are two different texts. The word is text of the page in two more ways (probed in Chromium 153 and Firefox 153): the text search of a browser finds it, and a selection over a chip carries it, "grün: in Betrieb" in Firefox and "grün:IN BETRIEB" in Chromium, which takes the label as it is shown. The hidden word is placed against its chip, which is `position:relative` for that reason alone. Placed against the page it does not scroll with the preview pane of the editor and makes the page behind the pane as high as the document, so that a click on an entry of the table of contents scrolls the toolbar out of the window; the comparison run checks both.

**A chip in a heading.** That word is part of neither the heading's entries nor its anchor. `## Bestellung` with the chip `🟢 Live` behind it is "Bestellung Live" in the table of contents and in both rails, and its anchor is `bestellung-live`; a heading that is only a chip is "Live" with the anchor `live`, never an empty entry. Both come from `headingLabelText()` in `src/app/toc.js`, which leaves the word out by the class `chips.js` exports. A chip that touches the text before it gets a blank there, one and no more: `## Bestellung:` with the chip directly behind the colon reads "Bestellung: Live" and keeps the anchor `bestellung-live`, because as a code span it held that blank itself, behind the dot. A chip with a blank before it, or at the start of the heading, gets none. The anchors are the ones these headings had before there were chips, when the dot fell out of the slug as every character does that is no letter or digit: a document that already wrote a status into a heading keeps its links, and no other anchor moved. In the entries the chip is its label as plain text, without pill and mark.

**What chips did to documents** (2026-10-02, `tests/vergleich.mjs --strict`, the built file before story 2.3 against the one after, Chromium 153 and Firefox 153, with the reference document as it was before the story and with the demo text, neither of which has a chip): all 136 screenshots are pixel-identical, and all assertions are green. Outside its `<style>` each of the twelve read-only exports is the file it was, once the ids Mermaid generates are masked (in this pair of runs the Chromium exports of the reference document fell on the two forms described under *The script's modules*); the stylesheet in it grew by 1 694 B, the rules for chips. The file itself and every `Mit Editor` file grew by 2 626 B (84 103 → 86 729 B). Then the reference document got its chips; with it all assertions are green in all four variants and both browsers, and its sizes are under *Download variants*. `tests/speichern.mjs` and `tests/durchlaeufe.mjs` are green in both browsers; a chip cannot fail, so the failure-case run has no case for it. Afterwards the demo text got a section on status chips (Ben, 2026-10-02), so that they can be seen and tried in the file as it opens: the five colours in running text, a table, a heading, bold text and a link. The file grew by another 903 B (86 729 → 87 632 B); with the new demo text all assertions are green in all four variants and both browsers, and so are the save round trip and the failure-case run. Then the labels got a darker shade than the marks (Ben, 2026-10-02), which gives each mark a colour of its own: another 122 B in the document styles of every variant (87 632 → 87 754 B), and all assertions green again with both documents. Three controls, each a copy of the sources built and run in Chromium on the new reference document: with the mark of blue drawn like the one of grey, and the word set to `display:none`, the run fails in every variant on the pictures of the marks ("grey and blue: 0 px"), on the word and on the accessible text; with `headingLabelText()` leaving the word in, it fails on the anchors (`automat-im-eingang-gruen-in-betrieb`) and on the entries; with the pass moved behind the table of contents, it fails on the entries, which then carry the dot.

**Known limits,** because the pass sees the markup and not the tokens, and because the convention borrows the code span.

- *A code span that only happens to start like a status becomes a chip.* `` `🔴 = Fehler` `` in a legend that explains the dots is meant as code and renders as a red chip reading "= Fehler". The way round it is anything before the dot; a blank is enough: `` ` 🔴 = Fehler` `` stays code. (A blank at both ends does not help: Markdown strips one from each end of a code span.) A legend can also put the dot outside the span.
- *A `<code>` element written as HTML with text alone becomes a chip too.* `<code>🟢 Live</code>` arrives as the markup of a code span.
- *The label is shown in capitals,* by `text-transform`. The text in the markup keeps its case, and so does the word for assistive technology; a browser may hand the label to a screen reader in capitals.

### Block markers, cards and step lists

A comment that starts with `dokufix:` applies a named component to the block that directly follows it. `cards` turns a bullet list into cards, `steps` a numbered list into a step list, `facets Spalte` puts a filter over a table, and `filter` gives a table a search field (see *Tables*).

```markdown
<!-- dokufix: cards -->
- **Selbstverbuchung** Am Automaten im Eingang, ohne Wartezeit.
- **Theke** Mit Beratung, zu den Öffnungszeiten.

<!-- dokufix: steps -->
1. *Leserin:* Medium auf die Ablage legen.
2. Beleg mitnehmen.
```

**The convention** is an HTML comment in the line above the block (decision D1, 2026-10-01). dokufix adds no syntax and no extension of `marked`: `marked` lets raw HTML through, so the comment arrives in the rendered document as a comment node in front of its block, and `applyMarkers()` in `src/app/markers.js`, the document pass `Markierungen`, works there. In a renderer that lets HTML comments through as well, GitHub among them, nothing of the marker shows and the block is what it is in Markdown: a plain list. That is why the comment was chosen over an attribute list (`{.cards}`) and a fenced div (`::: cards`), which both leave text on GitHub. It does not hold for every renderer: the review of the spike (`spikes/komponenten-aus-markdown/REVIEW.md`, open point 2) found that markdown-it in its default setting shows the comment as text and that MDX stops on it. Neither was measured again for this story.

**What is a marker, and what it applies to.**

| Source | Result |
|---|---|
| `<!-- dokufix: cards -->`, then a bullet list | cards; the comment is gone from the document |
| `<!-- dokufix: steps -->`, then a numbered list | a step list; the comment is gone |
| blank lines between the marker and its block | the same: only something that renders counts as "between" |
| another comment between the two, a marker or not | the same, so two markers one after the other both have the block behind them, and each takes effect or warns on its own |
| `<!--dokufix:cards-->`, `<!-- DOKUFIX: Cards -->`, a comment over several lines | a marker: blanks are free, and `dokufix:` and the name are read in any case |
| a marker in a list item, in a quotation, in a callout, in the line directly below a paragraph | it applies to the block that follows it there |
| `<!-- Notiz -->`, `<!-- dokufix cards -->`: a comment that does not start with `dokufix:` | no marker; it stays in the document as the comment it is |
| the marker in a code span or a code block, or masked (`\<!-- dokufix: cards -->`) | text, shown as written |

**A marker that cannot take effect** becomes the product's warning (`buildWarning()`, see *Render passes*) at its place, and its block stays what it was. There is no second kind of warning. Each text names the marker as it was written, on one line and with at most 80 characters of it.

| Source | The warning reads |
|---|---|
| `<!-- dokufix: crads -->`: a name that is not on the list | Unbekannte Markierung „dokufix: crads“. Bekannt sind: cards, steps, facets, filter. |
| `<!-- dokufix: -->`, `<!-- dokufix: "cards" -->`: no name that can be read, which is a letter followed by letters, digits and hyphens | Die Markierung „dokufix:“ nennt keine Komponente. Bekannt sind: cards, steps, facets, filter. |
| `steps` before a bullet list; `cards` before a table, a numbered list, a paragraph; `facets` or `filter` before a list or a paragraph; a marker with nothing behind it | Die Markierung „dokufix: steps“ erwartet direkt danach eine nummerierte Liste. For `cards`: … eine Aufzählung. For `facets` and `filter`: … eine Tabelle. |
| a paragraph, a heading or text on the marker's own line between the marker and the list | the same: the block is not directly behind the marker |
| `<!-- dokufix: cards zwei -->`: something behind the name of a marker that takes nothing | Die Markierung „dokufix: cards zwei“ nimmt keine Angabe hinter ihrem Namen. |
| a marker inside a paragraph or a heading, `Text <!-- dokufix: cards -->` | the warning of a marker with no block behind it: inside a paragraph no block can follow |
| the same marker a second time before one block, `<!-- dokufix: steps -->` twice above one list | Die Markierung „dokufix: steps“ steht mehr als einmal vor demselben Block. The component is applied once; every further marker of that name before the block is this warning |
| a component that refuses its block, and says why | Die Markierung „dokufix: facets Tpy“ nennt eine Spalte, die die Tabelle nicht hat. The reason is the component's (see *Tables* for the ones of `facets`); the block stays what it was, and nothing is on the console |
| a component that throws on its block | Die Markierung „dokufix: cards“ konnte nicht angewendet werden., with the message below it; the error is on the console |

- **No marker stops another.** The pass first reads every marker with the block behind it and then applies each or replaces it by its warning. A broken marker changes nothing for the ones before and after it, and two markers before one block both get that block whatever the first one does to it, also when the first moves it into a new parent, as `facets` does with its table. The call of a component stands in a containment of its own. So no marker reaches the containment of the pass runner, and the pass `Markierungen` does not fail because of what an author wrote.
- **Where the warning stands.** In the marker's place. The warning is a `<div>`, and a `<div>` inside a `<p>` ends the paragraph when an export is parsed again. So a marker that stands inside a paragraph, a heading or anything else that holds text only puts its warning after that element, and two markers in one paragraph keep their order.
- **An author cannot set a class through a marker.** Only a name of the list acts, and what it does is what its component does. The name is looked for in the list, not taken as a property of an object, so `<!-- dokufix: constructor -->` is an unknown name like any other; and what follows the name is handed to the component or refused, never written into the document. The spike wrote the name into `class` and the rest into a `data-` attribute (its review, rows 2 and 20).

**The list of markers** is `MARKERS` in `src/app/markers.js`, the one place the known markers stand in.

| Name | Block it expects, by tag | As a warning names the block (`BLOCKS`) | Argument | Its entry and what it does |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `cards` | `UL` | eine Aufzählung | none | `CARDS` and `buildCards()` in `src/app/cards.js` |
| `steps` | `OL` | eine nummerierte Liste | none | `STEPS` and `buildSteps()` in `src/app/steps.js` |
| `facets` | `TABLE` | eine Tabelle | text: the column | `FACETS` and `buildFacets()` in `src/app/facets.js` |
| `filter` | `TABLE` | eine Tabelle | text: the placeholder of the search field | `FILTER` and `markFilter()` in `src/app/filter.js`; the field is the run-time pass `Tabellenfilter` |

**A story that brings a marker** writes its component as a module of pure logic that exports one entry, `{ name, block, argument, apply }`, and adds the entry to `MARKERS`. `name` is what an author writes, in small letters. `block` is the tag of the block the marker expects directly behind it; a kind of block no marker expected so far gets its German word in `BLOCKS`, in the accusative, as it stands behind "erwartet direkt danach". `argument` says how what follows the name is read: `'none'`, the marker takes nothing and warns when something stands there, or `'text'`, it is handed to `apply(block, argument)` as `readMarker()` read it, which is everything behind the name without the blanks around it, and without the double quotes when it stands in a pair of them (`<!-- dokufix: name "Spalte A" -->`). `apply` acts, or, when it cannot act on this block, changes nothing and returns `{ warning: reason }`: the reason ends the sentence that starts `Die Markierung „…“`, and the marker becomes that warning at its place (`refusedMarker()`). A refusal is no failure and logs nothing; a component that throws is one. Nothing in `render.js` changes, and the warnings for an unknown name, a wrong block and a block that is not directly behind come with the list. The rest is the component's: its styles in `src/doc.css`, its cases in `tests/markers.test.mjs`, and what the comparison run expects of it in `markerExpectations()` of `tests/vergleich.mjs`, which stops on a marker it has no expectation for. `facets` is the first marker with `argument: 'text'` and the first that refuses a block; `tests/markers.test.mjs` drives both with a list of its own as well. Story 2.6 added `filter` this way, the second marker before a table, which may stand before the same table as `facets`; it never refuses a table. The comparison run asks `repeatedMarker()` for the warning of a marker that stands before a block a second time, so that the reference document can hold one.

**Cards.** The markup is the list itself with a class, `<ul class="dokufix-cards">`, and each item is a card. The title of a card is the bold text its item starts with: `<strong class="dokufix-card-title">`. Which element that is, is decided in the pass, by the class; the document styles style the class and never ask where a `<strong>` stands.

| Item | Title |
|---|---|
| `- **Titel** Text` | "Titel" |
| the same in a list with blank lines between its items, where `marked` wraps the content of an item in paragraphs | "Titel" |
| `- **Titel**: Text`, a colon directly behind the title | "Titel"; the colon is removed with the blanks around it, as the colon of a step's actor is: the title is a line of its own, and the colon would open the line below it. A colon inside the bold text, `**Titel:**`, stays |
| `- **Titel**`, two blanks or a backslash at the end of the line, then `Text`: a hard break behind the title | "Titel"; the break is removed, because the title is a line of its own already |
| `- Vorab **fett** mittendrin` | none: a card without a title |
| `- ***Titel*** Text`, `- [**Titel**](…) Text`, `- [ ] offen` | none: the item starts with something else |

**The look of cards** is in `src/doc.css`: a grid with columns of at least 210 px that puts as many cards beside each other as fit and wraps the rest, each card with a thin border, the title a line of its own. No colour of its own: the greys are those of the table of contents and the metadata panel. A list inside a card stays a list.

**Step lists.** The markup is `<ol class="dokufix-steps">`, and the pass writes two things into each item.

- *The number,* `<span class="dokufix-step-number">3</span>`, the first thing in the item. The list's own markers are switched off, and the number is shown as a small tile left of the step: it floats, pulled into the room the item's padding leaves. The item is not positioned, and neither is the tile. A positioned item would be the containing block of the preview of a footnote cited in the step, which has to be placed against the page (see *Footnotes*): measured in Chromium 153 at 900 px with the item positioned, as it first was, that preview lay over its own marker and the step. The comparison run holds the place of that preview. It is text in the markup and not a counter of the styles, for one reason: a counter starts at 1 whatever the list says, and the spike set it right with an inline style (its review, row 11). The pass works the number out as a browser does, from the start value of the list (`3.` as the first item gives `<ol start="3">`) and, for a list written as HTML, from a `value` attribute of an item. So a list that begins with 3 counts from 3, with no inline style. The list keeps its `start` attribute. Being text, the number is part of what a selection copies ("3", then the actor, then the step, each on a line of its own) and of the accessible text of its item, which stays an item of a list (looked at in Chromium 153, the second through Playwright's `ariaSnapshot()`; no check holds either).
- *The actor,* `<em class="dokufix-step-actor">Website</em>`: the emphasised text an item starts with, when it ends with a colon inside the emphasis. The colon is removed; the actor stands above the step as a small label.
- *In the preview of a footnote.* A step list inside a footnote definition is copied into that footnote's preview, which is one run of text and keeps `<span>` and `<em>` with their classes (see *Footnotes*). One rule of `src/doc.css` takes tile and label back there: number and actor read as plain text of the line, "1 Leserin Medium einlegen 2 Beleg mitnehmen".

| Item | Actor |
|---|---|
| `1. *Website:* Formular absenden` | "Website" |
| the same in a list with blank lines between its items | "Website" |
| `1. ***Website:*** …` | "Website", still bold |
| `1. *Nie* ohne Backup starten.` | none; the emphasis stays as it is |
| `1. *CRM*: Text`, the colon behind the emphasis | none |
| `1. Text *Website:*`, `1. **Fett:** Text` | none: not at the start, or not emphasis |

**What markers did to documents** (2026-10-02, `tests/vergleich.mjs --strict`, the built file before story 2.4 against the one after, Chromium 153 and Firefox 153, with the reference document and the demo text as they were before the story, neither of which has a marker): all 136 screenshots are pixel-identical, and all assertions are green. Outside its `<style>` each of the twelve read-only exports is the file it was, once the ids Mermaid generates are masked; the stylesheet in it grew by 1 458 B, the rules of cards and step lists. The file itself and every `Mit Editor` file grew by 5 485 B (87 754 → 93 239 B).
